Well, what brought me in is that my head feels so... foggy lately. I just keep mixing up simple things. Like appointment times, and exhibit dates for the local museum—I’ve been volunteering there since my husband passed, actually. He was a history teacher, you know, so he always loved museums. We used to go to this little one in Ohio that had the most beautiful collection of antique clocks. He could spend hours just listening to them tick. But anyway, yes, the dates. And I keep forgetting which volunteer I already called for the bake sale. I’ll dial the number and think, wait, didn't I just speak to Susan yesterday? Susan makes the most wonderful lemon bars, by the way. I asked her for the recipe once, but she said it was a family secret. Can you imagine? A secret recipe for lemon bars. But yes, my head just feels very cloudy.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think... I suppose this has been going on for about four months. Yes, it started right around the time the water damage pushed us into the remodel. A pipe just completely gave out in the downstairs bathroom. The plumber said it was the age of the house, but I've always thought it was the hard water we have in this town. It leaves the most awful white crust on my good drinking glasses. I have to soak them in white vinegar just to get them clear again. 

My mother actually taught me that trick. She swore by vinegar for absolutely everything—cleaning, cooking, even bee stings! She grew up during the Depression, you see, so they didn't have all these fancy chemical sprays we have now. You just used what was in the pantry and made do. She used to keep a big glass jug of it right next to the icebox. But oh, the dust from tearing up those wet floorboards for the remodel was just everywhere, it coated all my picture frames in the hallway. But yes, about four months of this cloudy feeling.

And I keep forgetting which volunteer I already called for the bake sale. I’ll dial the number and think, wait, didn't I just speak to Susan yesterday? I have to soak them in white vinegar just to get them clear again. \n\nMy mother actually taught me that trick. She swore by vinegar for absolutely everything—cleaning, cooking, even bee stings! She grew up during the Depression, you see, so they didn't have all these fancy chemical sprays we have now. You just used what was in the pantry and made do. Well, you know, it's funny you ask that. I suppose... well, I wouldn't really say sad or empty, no. I mean, Arthur passed five years ago this November, right around Thanksgiving. We actually didn't even have turkey that year, because I just couldn't bring myself to cook it. We had ham instead. My sister brought it over, a honey-baked one, and she left it on the porch because she didn't want to disturb me. She’s always been so considerate like that. She lives up in Michigan now, near Traverse City. Have you ever been? They have the most wonderful cherry festival in the summer. Arthur and I went once, back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We bought a whole crate of cherries and I made pies for a week straight. My hands were stained red for days! But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty lately. Just... foggy. Like I'm walking through a cloud of that remodel dust.

No, no, my memory from back then is sharp as a tack. I can remember Arthur's old stories, our wedding day, all of it. In fact, just the other day I was thinking about this one time we went to the state fair. It must have been... oh, 1975? Or maybe '76. Because that was the year they had that enormous prize-winning pumpkin. It was the size of a Volkswagen! I remember Arthur bought me a funnel cake, and we sat on this green bench near the Ferris wheel. They don't make funnel cakes like they used to, do they? Now they're all greasy and they skimp on the powdered sugar. I bought one at the county fair last summer and it just tasted like old oil. It gave me terrible heartburn. I had to take two antacids before bed. But anyway, no, those older memories are perfectly clear. It's just the recent things, like the bake sale and the museum dates, that get all jumbled up in the fog.

Well, yes, actually. It's been quite a bother. I've completely missed a few doctor visits lately, which is so unlike me. I've always kept a very tidy calendar. And the senior center schedule! Oh, I've tangled that up so badly. They have this wonderful water aerobics class on Tuesdays, but last week I showed up on Thursday for the knitting circle in my swimsuit. Mabel just looked at me like I'd grown a second head. She's always been a bit judgmental, though. I remember when she criticized my potato salad at the Fourth of July picnic back in '98. Said it had too much celery. Can you imagine? Celery gives it that perfect crunch! I buy the organic kind now, from that little farmer's market on Elm Street. The young man who runs the stall is so polite, he always carries my bags to the car. 

But anyway, because of all this confusion, my husband—well, I mean Arthur, bless his heart, wait, no, I mean... oh dear, my son-in-law, Robert, he's had to step in. Yes, Robert. He's been helping me with all the calls and keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the remodel, making all those repair decisions I used to handle just fine on my own. It's just... it's all so foggy.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the date? Sometimes, I suppose. It's funny you mention the time of day, though, because just yesterday morning—or was it afternoon? I was sitting on the porch with a cup of tea. Earl Grey, hot, with just a splash of milk. I can't drink coffee anymore, it makes my heart flutter like a trapped bird. Anyway, I was watching the neighbor's cat, that big orange tabby. Garfield, they call him. Such an unoriginal name, don't you think? Arthur and I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo. She had the bluest eyes, like little sapphires. We got her from a breeder in Ohio when we drove down for my cousin's wedding. It rained the entire trip, and the windshield wipers broke halfway there! We had to pull over at this little diner that served the most incredible cherry pie... oh, but what were we talking about? The date? I guess I do lose track of it sometimes in all this fog. But I always know when the mail comes, because the carrier, Jerry, whistles the same tune every day at exactly one o'clock.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Where I am? Oh, no, I always know where I am. I've lived in this town for... gosh, fifty years now? We bought the house on Maple Drive back in '72. Or was it '73? It was right after Arthur got his promotion at the bank. He was so proud. He came home with a bouquet of yellow roses—my favorite—and a bottle of champagne. We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.

calls, calendars, and repair decisions I used to handle myself.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about four months, since the water damage pushed us into the remodel.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 3 months\n  manifestation: The low, slowed-down part has been with me for about three months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 79 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing your daily tasks because of this foggy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's been quite a bother. I've completely missed a few doctor visits lately, which is so unlike me. I've always kept a very tidy calendar. And the senior center schedule! Oh, I've tangled that up so badly. They have this wonderful water aerobics class on Tuesdays, but last week I showed up on Thursday for the knitting circle in my swimsuit. Mabel just looked at me like I'd grown a second head. She's always been a bit judgmental, though. I remember when she criticized my potato salad at the Fourth of July picnic back in '98. Said it had too much celery. Can you imagine? Celery gives it that perfect crunch! I buy the organic kind now, from that little farmer's market on Elm Street. The young man who runs the stall is so polite, he always carries my bags to the car. \n\nBut anyway, because of all this confusion, my husband—well, I mean Arthur, bless his heart, wait, no, I mean... oh dear, my son-in-law, Robert, he's had to step in. Yes, Robert. He's been helping me with all the calls and keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the remodel, making all those repair decisions I used to handle just fine on my own. It's just... it's all so foggy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure of the date or time of day?\n\nPatient: Well, the date? Sometimes, I suppose. It's funny you mention the time of day, though, because just yesterday morning—or was it afternoon? I was sitting on the porch with a cup of tea. Earl Grey, hot, with just a splash of milk. I can't drink coffee anymore, it makes my heart flutter like a trapped bird. Anyway, I was watching the neighbor's cat, that big orange tabby. Garfield, they call him. Such an unoriginal name, don't you think? Arthur and I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo. She had the bluest eyes, like little sapphires. We got her from a breeder in Ohio when we drove down for my cousin's wedding. It rained the entire trip, and the windshield wipers broke halfway there! We had to pull over at this little diner that served the most incredible cherry pie... oh, but what were we talking about? The date? I guess I do lose track of it sometimes in all this fog. But I always know when the mail comes, because the carrier, Jerry, whistles the same tune every day at exactly one o'clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unsure about where you are?\n\nPatient: Where I am? Oh, no, I always know where I am. I've lived in this town for... gosh, fifty years now? We bought the house on Maple Drive back in '72. Or was it '73? It was right after Arthur got his promotion at the bank. He was so proud. He came home with a bouquet of yellow roses—my favorite—and a bottle of champagne. We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. But sometimes they do all blend together, especially since Arthur passed. When he was working, Monday felt like Monday, you know? He'd always have his blue tie on for the Monday staff meeting. He had this whole collection of ties. My favorite was the silk one with the little golf clubs on it that the kids got him for Father's Day. Oh, the kids... they grow up so fast. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The smoke alarm was screaming, and the fire department had to come. The firemen were very handsome, though. One of them had a mustache just like Tom Selleck. Remember Magnum P.I.? I used to watch that every Thursday night. Or was it Wednesday? Goodness, I can't remember what night that was on. But anyway, what day is it today? I guess I do get a little muddled on the days sometimes.

And I keep forgetting which volunteer I already called for the bake sale. I’ll dial the number and think, wait, didn't I just speak to Susan yesterday? Susan makes the most wonderful lemon bars, by the way. I asked her for the recipe once, but she said it was a family secret. Can you imagine? A secret recipe for lemon bars. I suppose this has been going on for about four months. Yes, it started right around the time the water damage pushed us into the remodel. A pipe just completely gave out in the downstairs bathroom. The plumber said it was the age of the house, but I've always thought it was the hard water we have in this town. It leaves the most awful white crust on my good drinking glasses. I have to soak them in white vinegar just to get them clear again. \n\nMy mother actually taught me that trick. She swore by vinegar for absolutely everything—cleaning, cooking, even bee stings! She grew up during the Depression, you see, so they didn't have all these fancy chemical sprays we have now. You just used what was in the pantry and made do. We actually didn't even have turkey that year, because I just couldn't bring myself to cook it. We had ham instead. My sister brought it over, a honey-baked one, and she left it on the porch because she didn't want to disturb me. She’s always been so considerate like that. She lives up in Michigan now, near Traverse City. Have you ever been? They have the most wonderful cherry festival in the summer. Arthur and I went once, back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We bought a whole crate of cherries and I made pies for a week straight. My hands were stained red for days! But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty lately. Just... foggy. Like I'm walking through a cloud of that remodel dust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: No, no, my memory from back then is sharp as a tack. Because that was the year they had that enormous prize-winning pumpkin. It was the size of a Volkswagen! We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nPatient: Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The time of day? Oh, no, I usually know that. I mean, the sun is a pretty good indicator, isn't it? Unless it's winter. Winter afternoons get so dark so early, it feels like midnight at five o'clock. I always hated that. Arthur used to come home in the pitch black, and we'd have dinner by lamplight. I had this beautiful Tiffany-style lamp on the dining table. Stained glass, with all these rich greens and reds. I bought it at an estate sale over in Westover. The woman running the sale had the most enormous collection of vintage brooches. I almost bought one shaped like a peacock, but I didn't have enough cash on me. You know, nobody carries cash anymore. It's all those plastic cards. When I go to the grocery store, the young people just tap their phones on the machine! It's like magic, or science fiction. I still write a check for my groceries. The cashier at the Piggly Wiggly—her name is Brenda, lovely girl, just had a baby boy—she's always so patient with me while I fill it out. But time of day? No, I don't really lose track of that. My stomach always tells me when it's time for lunch, anyway.

We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? Learning new things? No, I wouldn't say that. I do just fine. I mean, my grandson Tommy got me one of those... what do you call it, a tablet? For Christmas. And I keep forgetting which volunteer I already called for the bake sale. I’ll dial the number and think, wait, didn't I just speak to Susan yesterday? Susan makes the most wonderful lemon bars, by the way. I asked her for the recipe once, but she said it was a family secret. Can you imagine? A secret recipe for lemon bars. But yes, my head just feels very cloudy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this foggy sensation in your head?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose this has been going on for about four months. Yes, it started right around the time the water damage pushed us into the remodel. A pipe just completely gave out in the downstairs bathroom. The plumber said it was the age of the house, but I've always thought it was the hard water we have in this town. It leaves the most awful white crust on my good drinking glasses. I have to soak them in white vinegar just to get them clear again. \n\nMy mother actually taught me that trick. She swore by vinegar for absolutely everything—cleaning, cooking, even bee stings! She grew up during the Depression, you see, so they didn't have all these fancy chemical sprays we have now. You just used what was in the pantry and made do. She used to keep a big glass jug of it right next to the icebox. But oh, the dust from tearing up those wet floorboards for the remodel was just everywhere, it coated all my picture frames in the hallway. But yes, about four months of this cloudy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty since your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that. I suppose... well, I wouldn't really say sad or empty, no. I mean, Arthur passed five years ago this November, right around Thanksgiving. We actually didn't even have turkey that year, because I just couldn't bring myself to cook it. We had ham instead. My sister brought it over, a honey-baked one, and she left it on the porch because she didn't want to disturb me. She’s always been so considerate like that. She lives up in Michigan now, near Traverse City. Have you ever been? They have the most wonderful cherry festival in the summer. Arthur and I went once, back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We bought a whole crate of cherries and I made pies for a week straight. My hands were stained red for days! But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty lately. Just... foggy. Like I'm walking through a cloud of that remodel dust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: No, no, my memory from back then is sharp as a tack. I can remember Arthur's old stories, our wedding day, all of it. In fact, just the other day I was thinking about this one time we went to the state fair. It must have been... oh, 1975? Or maybe '76. Because that was the year they had that enormous prize-winning pumpkin. It was the size of a Volkswagen! I remember Arthur bought me a funnel cake, and we sat on this green bench near the Ferris wheel. They don't make funnel cakes like they used to, do they? Now they're all greasy and they skimp on the powdered sugar. I bought one at the county fair last summer and it just tasted like old oil. It gave me terrible heartburn. I had to take two antacids before bed. But anyway, no, those older memories are perfectly clear. It's just the recent things, like the bake sale and the museum dates, that get all jumbled up in the fog.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing your daily tasks because of this foggy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's been quite a bother. I've completely missed a few doctor visits lately, which is so unlike me. I've always kept a very tidy calendar. And the senior center schedule! Oh, I've tangled that up so badly. They have this wonderful water aerobics class on Tuesdays, but last week I showed up on Thursday for the knitting circle in my swimsuit. Mabel just looked at me like I'd grown a second head. She's always been a bit judgmental, though. I remember when she criticized my potato salad at the Fourth of July picnic back in '98. Said it had too much celery. Can you imagine? Celery gives it that perfect crunch! I buy the organic kind now, from that little farmer's market on Elm Street. The young man who runs the stall is so polite, he always carries my bags to the car. \n\nBut anyway, because of all this confusion, my husband—well, I mean Arthur, bless his heart, wait, no, I mean... oh dear, my son-in-law, Robert, he's had to step in. Yes, Robert. He's been helping me with all the calls and keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the remodel, making all those repair decisions I used to handle just fine on my own. It's just... it's all so foggy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure of the date or time of day?\n\nPatient: Well, the date? Sometimes, I suppose. It's funny you mention the time of day, though, because just yesterday morning—or was it afternoon? I was sitting on the porch with a cup of tea. Earl Grey, hot, with just a splash of milk. I can't drink coffee anymore, it makes my heart flutter like a trapped bird. Anyway, I was watching the neighbor's cat, that big orange tabby. Garfield, they call him. Such an unoriginal name, don't you think? Arthur and I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo. She had the bluest eyes, like little sapphires. We got her from a breeder in Ohio when we drove down for my cousin's wedding. It rained the entire trip, and the windshield wipers broke halfway there! We had to pull over at this little diner that served the most incredible cherry pie... oh, but what were we talking about? The date? I guess I do lose track of it sometimes in all this fog. But I always know when the mail comes, because the carrier, Jerry, whistles the same tune every day at exactly one o'clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unsure about where you are?\n\nPatient: Where I am? Oh, no, I always know where I am. I've lived in this town for... gosh, fifty years now? We bought the house on Maple Drive back in '72. Or was it '73? It was right after Arthur got his promotion at the bank. He was so proud. He came home with a bouquet of yellow roses—my favorite—and a bottle of champagne. We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nPatient: Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. But sometimes they do all blend together, especially since Arthur passed. When he was working, Monday felt like Monday, you know? He'd always have his blue tie on for the Monday staff meeting. He had this whole collection of ties. My favorite was the silk one with the little golf clubs on it that the kids got him for Father's Day. Oh, the kids... they grow up so fast. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The smoke alarm was screaming, and the fire department had to come. The firemen were very handsome, though. One of them had a mustache just like Tom Selleck. Remember Magnum P.I.? I used to watch that every Thursday night. Or was it Wednesday? Goodness, I can't remember what night that was on. But anyway, what day is it today? I guess I do get a little muddled on the days sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: The time of day? Oh, no, I usually know that. I mean, the sun is a pretty good indicator, isn't it? Unless it's winter. Winter afternoons get so dark so early, it feels like midnight at five o'clock. I always hated that. Arthur used to come home in the pitch black, and we'd have dinner by lamplight. I had this beautiful Tiffany-style lamp on the dining table. Stained glass, with all these rich greens and reds. I bought it at an estate sale over in Westover. The woman running the sale had the most enormous collection of vintage brooches. I almost bought one shaped like a peacock, but I didn't have enough cash on me. You know, nobody carries cash anymore. It's all those plastic cards. When I go to the grocery store, the young people just tap their phones on the machine! It's like magic, or science fiction. I still write a check for my groceries. The cashier at the Piggly Wiggly—her name is Brenda, lovely girl, just had a baby boy—she's always so patient with me while I fill it out. But time of day? No, I don't really lose track of that. My stomach always tells me when it's time for lunch, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble learning new things, like remembering new names or how to use new devices?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I wouldn't say that. I do just fine. I mean, my grandson Tommy got me one of those... what do you call it, a tablet? For Christmas. He wrapped it in this beautiful shiny silver foil paper. I always save the good wrapping paper, you know. My mother taught me that. We grew up when you didn't just throw things away! We'd actually iron the wrapping paper flat and fold it up to keep in the cedar chest. Oh, that chest smelled so wonderful. It sat right at the foot of my bed. I shared a room with my sister, Betty. Betty was always getting into my things, especially my makeup. She actually took my favorite red lipstick right before the spring dance in... oh, it must have been 1962. I was so mad at her! I had to wear this awful pale pink color that just washed me out completely. I did have a gorgeous blue dress, though, with a sweetheart neckline. My aunt made it for me. She was a seamstress downtown, right next to the old bakery that sold those wonderful almond cookies. I haven't had one of those in years. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, new things. No, I don't have any trouble, I just ask Tommy if I need help with the tablet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F32.0", "G31.84", "R41.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

My stomach always tells me when it's time for lunch, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble learning new things, like remembering new names or how to use new devices?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I wouldn't say that. I do just fine. I mean, my grandson Tommy got me one of those... what do you call it, a tablet? For Christmas. He wrapped it in this beautiful shiny silver foil paper. I always save the good wrapping paper, you know. My mother taught me that. We grew up when you didn't just throw things away! We'd actually iron the wrapping paper flat and fold it up to keep in the cedar chest. Oh, that chest smelled so wonderful. It sat right at the foot of my bed. I shared a room with my sister, Betty. Betty was always getting into my things, especially my makeup. She actually took my favorite red lipstick right before the spring dance in... oh, it must have been 1962. I was so mad at her! I had to wear this awful pale pink color that just washed me out completely. I did have a gorgeous blue dress, though, with a sweetheart neckline. My aunt made it for me. She was a seamstress downtown, right next to the old bakery that sold those wonderful almond cookies. I haven't had one of those in years. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, new things. No, I don't have any trouble, I just ask Tommy if I need help with the tablet.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself unsure who you are or what your role is?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar I Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nPatient: Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. But sometimes they do all blend together, especially since Arthur passed. When he was working, Monday felt like Monday, you know? He'd always have his blue tie on for the Monday staff meeting. He had this whole collection of ties. My favorite was the silk one with the little golf clubs on it that the kids got him for Father's Day. Oh, the kids... they grow up so fast. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The smoke alarm was screaming, and the fire department had to come. The firemen were very handsome, though. One of them had a mustache just like Tom Selleck. Remember Magnum P.I.? I used to watch that every Thursday night. Or was it Wednesday? Goodness, I can't remember what night that was on. But anyway, what day is it today? I guess I do get a little muddled on the days sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: The time of day? Oh, no, I usually know that. I mean, the sun is a pretty good indicator, isn't it? Unless it's winter. Winter afternoons get so dark so early, it feels like midnight at five o'clock. I always hated that. Arthur used to come home in the pitch black, and we'd have dinner by lamplight. I had this beautiful Tiffany-style lamp on the dining table. Stained glass, with all these rich greens and reds. I bought it at an estate sale over in Westover. The woman running the sale had the most enormous collection of vintage brooches. I almost bought one shaped like a peacock, but I didn't have enough cash on me. You know, nobody carries cash anymore. It's all those plastic cards.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unsure who I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly who I am. I'm Eleanor, Arthur's wife, Tommy's grandmother. I've always known my place in the world. Although, the world certainly has changed, hasn't it? I remember when being a mother and a wife was all a woman really needed to worry about. We had such a lovely neighborhood back then. The Johnsons lived next door, and Mrs. Johnson—Edith, her name was—she used to grow the most spectacular hydrangeas. Huge, blue blooms, bigger than my head! She told me her secret was putting rusty nails in the soil. Can you imagine? Rusty nails! Arthur used to keep a coffee can full of rusty nails in the garage. He never threw anything away, just like me with the wrapping paper. His workbench was always covered in sawdust and little bits of wire. He built a birdhouse once, painted it bright red to attract the cardinals. We used to sit on the porch with our iced tea and watch them. I always put a sprig of fresh mint in my iced tea. Mint grows like a weed, you know. If you don't keep it contained in a pot, it'll take over your whole garden before you can blink. Just like the ivy on the old library downtown. They had to tear that building down, which was a crying shame. It had those beautiful stone gargoyles on the roof. But no, I've never forgotten who I am. I'm still just me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, G31.84, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brought me in is that my head feels so... foggy lately. I just keep mixing up simple things. Like appointment times, and exhibit dates for the local museum—I’ve been volunteering there since my husband passed, actually. He was a history teacher, you know, so he always loved museums. We used to go to this little one in Ohio that had the most beautiful collection of antique clocks. He could spend hours just listening to them tick. But anyway, yes, the dates. And I keep forgetting which volunteer I already called for the bake sale. I’ll dial the number and think, wait, didn't I just speak to Susan yesterday? Susan makes the most wonderful lemon bars, by the way. I asked her for the recipe once, but she said it was a family secret. Can you imagine? A secret recipe for lemon bars. But yes, my head just feels very cloudy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this foggy sensation in your head?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose this has been going on for about four months. Yes, it started right around the time the water damage pushed us into the remodel. A pipe just completely gave out in the downstairs bathroom. The plumber said it was the age of the house, but I've always thought it was the hard water we have in this town. It leaves the most awful white crust on my good drinking glasses. I have to soak them in white vinegar just to get them clear again. \n\nMy mother actually taught me that trick. She swore by vinegar for absolutely everything—cleaning, cooking, even bee stings! She grew up during the Depression, you see, so they didn't have all these fancy chemical sprays we have now. You just used what was in the pantry and made do. She used to keep a big glass jug of it right next to the icebox. But oh, the dust from tearing up those wet floorboards for the remodel was just everywhere, it coated all my picture frames in the hallway. But yes, about four months of this cloudy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty since your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that. I suppose... well, I wouldn't really say sad or empty, no. I mean, Arthur passed five years ago this November, right around Thanksgiving. We actually didn't even have turkey that year, because I just couldn't bring myself to cook it. We had ham instead. My sister brought it over, a honey-baked one, and she left it on the porch because she didn't want to disturb me. She’s always been so considerate like that. She lives up in Michigan now, near Traverse City. Have you ever been? They have the most wonderful cherry festival in the summer. Arthur and I went once, back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We bought a whole crate of cherries and I made pies for a week straight. My hands were stained red for days! But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty lately. Just... foggy. Like I'm walking through a cloud of that remodel dust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: No, no, my memory from back then is sharp as a tack. I can remember Arthur's old stories, our wedding day, all of it. In fact, just the other day I was thinking about this one time we went to the state fair. It must have been... oh, 1975? Or maybe '76. Because that was the year they had that enormous prize-winning pumpkin. It was the size of a Volkswagen! I remember Arthur bought me a funnel cake, and we sat on this green bench near the Ferris wheel. They don't make funnel cakes like they used to, do they? Now they're all greasy and they skimp on the powdered sugar. I bought one at the county fair last summer and it just tasted like old oil. It gave me terrible heartburn. I had to take two antacids before bed. But anyway, no, those older memories are perfectly clear. It's just the recent things, like the bake sale and the museum dates, that get all jumbled up in the fog.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing your daily tasks because of this foggy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's been quite a bother. I've completely missed a few doctor visits lately, which is so unlike me. I've always kept a very tidy calendar. And the senior center schedule! Oh, I've tangled that up so badly. They have this wonderful water aerobics class on Tuesdays, but last week I showed up on Thursday for the knitting circle in my swimsuit. Mabel just looked at me like I'd grown a second head. She's always been a bit judgmental, though. I remember when she criticized my potato salad at the Fourth of July picnic back in '98. Said it had too much celery. Can you imagine? Celery gives it that perfect crunch! I buy the organic kind now, from that little farmer's market on Elm Street. The young man who runs the stall is so polite, he always carries my bags to the car. \n\nBut anyway, because of all this confusion, my husband—well, I mean Arthur, bless his heart, wait, no, I mean... oh dear, my son-in-law, Robert, he's had to step in. Yes, Robert. He's been helping me with all the calls and keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the remodel, making all those repair decisions I used to handle just fine on my own. It's just... it's all so foggy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure of the date or time of day?\n\nPatient: Well, the date? Sometimes, I suppose. It's funny you mention the time of day, though, because just yesterday morning—or was it afternoon? I was sitting on the porch with a cup of tea. Earl Grey, hot, with just a splash of milk. I can't drink coffee anymore, it makes my heart flutter like a trapped bird. Anyway, I was watching the neighbor's cat, that big orange tabby. Garfield, they call him. Such an unoriginal name, don't you think? Arthur and I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo. She had the bluest eyes, like little sapphires. We got her from a breeder in Ohio when we drove down for my cousin's wedding. It rained the entire trip, and the windshield wipers broke halfway there! We had to pull over at this little diner that served the most incredible cherry pie... oh, but what were we talking about? The date? I guess I do lose track of it sometimes in all this fog. But I always know when the mail comes, because the carrier, Jerry, whistles the same tune every day at exactly one o'clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unsure about where you are?\n\nPatient: Where I am? Oh, no, I always know where I am. I've lived in this town for... gosh, fifty years now? We bought the house on Maple Drive back in '72. Or was it '73? It was right after Arthur got his promotion at the bank. He was so proud. He came home with a bouquet of yellow roses—my favorite—and a bottle of champagne. We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nPatient: Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. But sometimes they do all blend together, especially since Arthur passed. When he was working, Monday felt like Monday, you know? He'd always have his blue tie on for the Monday staff meeting. He had this whole collection of ties. My favorite was the silk one with the little golf clubs on it that the kids got him for Father's Day. Oh, the kids... they grow up so fast. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The smoke alarm was screaming, and the fire department had to come. The firemen were very handsome, though. One of them had a mustache just like Tom Selleck. Remember Magnum P.I.? I used to watch that every Thursday night. Or was it Wednesday? Goodness, I can't remember what night that was on. But anyway, what day is it today? I guess I do get a little muddled on the days sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: The time of day? Oh, no, I usually know that. I mean, the sun is a pretty good indicator, isn't it? Unless it's winter. Winter afternoons get so dark so early, it feels like midnight at five o'clock. I always hated that. Arthur used to come home in the pitch black, and we'd have dinner by lamplight. I had this beautiful Tiffany-style lamp on the dining table. Stained glass, with all these rich greens and reds. Such an unoriginal name, don't you think? Arthur and I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo. She had the bluest eyes, like little sapphires. We got her from a breeder in Ohio when we drove down for my cousin's wedding. It rained the entire trip, and the windshield wipers broke halfway there! We had to pull over at this little diner that served the most incredible cherry pie... oh, but what were we talking about? The date? I guess I do lose track of it sometimes in all this fog. But I always know when the mail comes, because the carrier, Jerry, whistles the same tune every day at exactly one o'clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unsure about where you are?\n\nPatient: Where I am? Oh, no, I always know where I am. I've lived in this town for... gosh, fifty years now? We bought the house on Maple Drive back in '72. Or was it '73? It was right after Arthur got his promotion at the bank. He was so proud. He came home with a bouquet of yellow roses—my favorite—and a bottle of champagne. We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nPatient: Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. But sometimes they do all blend together, especially since Arthur passed. When he was working, Monday felt like Monday, you know? He'd always have his blue tie on for the Monday staff meeting. He had this whole collection of ties. My favorite was the silk one with the little golf clubs on it that the kids got him for Father's Day. Oh, the kids... they grow up so fast. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The smoke alarm was screaming, and the fire department had to come. The firemen were very handsome, though. One of them had a mustache just like Tom Selleck. Remember Magnum P.I.? I used to watch that every Thursday night. Or was it Wednesday? Goodness, I can't remember what night that was on. But anyway, what day is it today? I guess I do get a little muddled on the days sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: The time of day? Oh, no, I usually know that. I mean, the sun is a pretty good indicator, isn't it? Unless it's winter. Winter afternoons get so dark so early, it feels like midnight at five o'clock. I always hated that. Arthur used to come home in the pitch black, and we'd have dinner by lamplight. I had this beautiful Tiffany-style lamp on the dining table. Stained glass, with all these rich greens and reds. I bought it at an estate sale over in Westover. The woman running the sale had the most enormous collection of vintage brooches. I almost bought one shaped like a peacock, but I didn't have enough cash on me. You know, nobody carries cash anymore. It's all those plastic cards. When I go to the grocery store, the young people just tap their phones on the machine! It's like magic, or science fiction. I still write a check for my groceries. The cashier at the Piggly Wiggly—her name is Brenda, lovely girl, just had a baby boy—she's always so patient with me while I fill it out. But time of day? No, I don't really lose track of that. My stomach always tells me when it's time for lunch, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble learning new things, like remembering new names or how to use new devices?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I wouldn't say that. I do just fine. I mean, my grandson Tommy got me one of those... what do you call it, a tablet? For Christmas. He wrapped it in this beautiful shiny silver foil paper. I always save the good wrapping paper, you know. My mother taught me that. We grew up when you didn't just throw things away! We'd actually iron the wrapping paper flat and fold it up to keep in the cedar chest. Oh, that chest smelled so wonderful. It sat right at the foot of my bed. I shared a room with my sister, Betty. Betty was always getting into my things, especially my makeup. She actually took my favorite red lipstick right before the spring dance in... oh, it must have been 1962. I was so mad at her! I had to wear this awful pale pink color that just washed me out completely. I did have a gorgeous blue dress, though, with a sweetheart neckline. My aunt made it for me. She was a seamstress downtown, right next to the old bakery that sold those wonderful almond cookies. I haven't had one of those in years. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, new things. No, I don't have any trouble, I just ask Tommy if I need help with the tablet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure who you are or what your role is?\n\nPatient: Unsure who I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly who I am. I'm Eleanor, Arthur's wife, Tommy's grandmother. I've always known my place in the world. Although, the world certainly has changed, hasn't it? I remember when being a mother and a wife was all a woman really needed to worry about. We had such a lovely neighborhood back then. The Johnsons lived next door, and Mrs. Johnson—Edith, her name was—she used to grow the most spectacular hydrangeas. Huge, blue blooms, bigger than my head! She told me her secret was putting rusty nails in the soil. Can you imagine? Rusty nails! Arthur used to keep a coffee can full of rusty nails in the garage. He never threw anything away, just like me with the wrapping paper. His workbench was always covered in sawdust and little bits of wire. He built a birdhouse once, painted it bright red to attract the cardinals. We used to sit on the porch with our iced tea and watch them. I always put a sprig of fresh mint in my iced tea. Mint grows like a weed, you know. If you don't keep it contained in a pot, it'll take over your whole garden before you can blink. Just like the ivy on the old library downtown. They had to tear that building down, which was a crying shame. It had those beautiful stone gargoyles on the roof. But no, I've never forgotten who I am. I'm still just me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I think you just asked me that, didn't you? About knowing who I am? Oh, it's alright, dear, I know you doctors have a lot of questions to get through. Arthur's doctor used to have this clipboard with pages and pages of questions. Dr. Henderson, his name was. He had the most magnificent mustache, curled right up at the ends like a man in a barbershop quartet. My father was in a barbershop quartet, actually! They called themselves the Melody Men. They'd practice in our living room on Thursday nights, and my mother would make them little cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off. She had a special silver tray she only used for company. I still have that tray, it's wrapped in felt in the dining room buffet. The buffet is real mahogany, they don't make furniture like that anymore. Now it's all that particle board you have to put together yourself with a tiny little wrench. Tommy put together a bookshelf for me last year and he was cursing a blue streak under his breath, though he thought I couldn't hear him. My hearing is still sharp as a tack, you know! I can hear the mail truck coming from three blocks away. But to answer your question again, no, I know exactly who I am. I haven't forgotten that.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, G31.84, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brought me in is that my head feels so... foggy lately. I just keep mixing up simple things. Like appointment times, and exhibit dates for the local museum—I’ve been volunteering there since my husband passed, actually. He was a history teacher, you know, so he always loved museums. We used to go to this little one in Ohio that had the most beautiful collection of antique clocks. He could spend hours just listening to them tick. But anyway, yes, the dates. And I keep forgetting which volunteer I already called for the bake sale. I’ll dial the number and think, wait, didn't I just speak to Susan yesterday? Susan makes the most wonderful lemon bars, by the way. I asked her for the recipe once, but she said it was a family secret. Can you imagine? A secret recipe for lemon bars. But yes, my head just feels very cloudy.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this foggy sensation in your head?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... I suppose this has been going on for about four months. Yes, it started right around the time the water damage pushed us into the remodel. A pipe just completely gave out in the downstairs bathroom. The plumber said it was the age of the house, but I've always thought it was the hard water we have in this town. It leaves the most awful white crust on my good drinking glasses. I have to soak them in white vinegar just to get them clear again. \n\nMy mother actually taught me that trick. She swore by vinegar for absolutely everything—cleaning, cooking, even bee stings! She grew up during the Depression, you see, so they didn't have all these fancy chemical sprays we have now. You just used what was in the pantry and made do. She used to keep a big glass jug of it right next to the icebox. But oh, the dust from tearing up those wet floorboards for the remodel was just everywhere, it coated all my picture frames in the hallway. But yes, about four months of this cloudy feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty since your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that. I suppose... well, I wouldn't really say sad or empty, no. I mean, Arthur passed five years ago this November, right around Thanksgiving. We actually didn't even have turkey that year, because I just couldn't bring myself to cook it. We had ham instead. My sister brought it over, a honey-baked one, and she left it on the porch because she didn't want to disturb me. She’s always been so considerate like that. She lives up in Michigan now, near Traverse City. Have you ever been? They have the most wonderful cherry festival in the summer. Arthur and I went once, back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We bought a whole crate of cherries and I made pies for a week straight. My hands were stained red for days! But no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or empty lately. Just... foggy. Like I'm walking through a cloud of that remodel dust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your husband passed away?\n\nPatient: No, no, my memory from back then is sharp as a tack. I can remember Arthur's old stories, our wedding day, all of it. In fact, just the other day I was thinking about this one time we went to the state fair. It must have been... oh, 1975? Or maybe '76. Because that was the year they had that enormous prize-winning pumpkin. It was the size of a Volkswagen! I remember Arthur bought me a funnel cake, and we sat on this green bench near the Ferris wheel. They don't make funnel cakes like they used to, do they? Now they're all greasy and they skimp on the powdered sugar. I bought one at the county fair last summer and it just tasted like old oil. It gave me terrible heartburn. I had to take two antacids before bed. But anyway, no, those older memories are perfectly clear. It's just the recent things, like the bake sale and the museum dates, that get all jumbled up in the fog.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing your daily tasks because of this foggy feeling?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. It's been quite a bother. I've completely missed a few doctor visits lately, which is so unlike me. I've always kept a very tidy calendar. And the senior center schedule! Oh, I've tangled that up so badly. They have this wonderful water aerobics class on Tuesdays, but last week I showed up on Thursday for the knitting circle in my swimsuit. Mabel just looked at me like I'd grown a second head. She's always been a bit judgmental, though. I remember when she criticized my potato salad at the Fourth of July picnic back in '98. Said it had too much celery. Can you imagine? Celery gives it that perfect crunch! I buy the organic kind now, from that little farmer's market on Elm Street. The young man who runs the stall is so polite, he always carries my bags to the car. \n\nBut anyway, because of all this confusion, my husband—well, I mean Arthur, bless his heart, wait, no, I mean... oh dear, my son-in-law, Robert, he's had to step in. Yes, Robert. He's been helping me with all the calls and keeping the calendar straight. He even had to take over dealing with the contractors for the remodel, making all those repair decisions I used to handle just fine on my own. It's just... it's all so foggy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure of the date or time of day?\n\nPatient: Well, the date? Sometimes, I suppose. It's funny you mention the time of day, though, because just yesterday morning—or was it afternoon? I was sitting on the porch with a cup of tea. Earl Grey, hot, with just a splash of milk. I can't drink coffee anymore, it makes my heart flutter like a trapped bird. Anyway, I was watching the neighbor's cat, that big orange tabby. Garfield, they call him. Such an unoriginal name, don't you think? Arthur and I had a cat once, a Siamese named Cleo. She had the bluest eyes, like little sapphires. We got her from a breeder in Ohio when we drove down for my cousin's wedding. It rained the entire trip, and the windshield wipers broke halfway there! We had to pull over at this little diner that served the most incredible cherry pie... oh, but what were we talking about? The date? I guess I do lose track of it sometimes in all this fog. But I always know when the mail comes, because the carrier, Jerry, whistles the same tune every day at exactly one o'clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel unsure about where you are?\n\nPatient: Where I am? Oh, no, I always know where I am. I've lived in this town for... gosh, fifty years now? We bought the house on Maple Drive back in '72. Or was it '73? It was right after Arthur got his promotion at the bank. He was so proud. He came home with a bouquet of yellow roses—my favorite—and a bottle of champagne. We didn't even have proper champagne flutes, so we drank it out of those little juice glasses we got for free at the gas station. Remember when gas stations used to give away glassware? You'd fill up your tank and get a lovely little tumbler with a painted daisy on it. I had a whole set of them, but over the years they got chipped or lost. I think my daughter took a few with her when she went off to college. She went to the state university, you know. Studied art history. I always wondered what she was going to do with a degree like that, but she ended up working at that lovely museum downtown. The one with the beautiful courtyard full of those pink dogwood trees. They bloom so beautifully in the spring, unless there's a late frost, which just ruins the blossoms. But anyway, no, I don't get lost or forget where I am. I know my way around just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what day of the week it is?\n\nPatient: Well, the day of the week? I suppose... I mean, mostly I know. But sometimes they do all blend together, especially since Arthur passed. When he was working, Monday felt like Monday, you know? He'd always have his blue tie on for the Monday staff meeting. He had this whole collection of ties. My favorite was the silk one with the little golf clubs on it that the kids got him for Father's Day. Oh, the kids... they grow up so fast. Robert, my son-in-law, he tries to come by on Sundays to check the gutters or whatever needs fixing. He's very handy. Not like my brother Frank. Frank couldn't even change a lightbulb without making a fuss. He tried to fix my toaster once in '85 and nearly burned the kitchen down! The smoke alarm was screaming, and the fire department had to come. The firemen were very handsome, though. One of them had a mustache just like Tom Selleck. Remember Magnum P.I.? I used to watch that every Thursday night. Or was it Wednesday? Goodness, I can't remember what night that was on. But anyway, what day is it today? I guess I do get a little muddled on the days sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself unsure what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: The time of day? Oh, no, I usually know that. I mean, the sun is a pretty good indicator, isn't it? Unless it's winter. Winter afternoons get so dark so early, it feels like midnight at five o'clock. I always hated that. Arthur used to come home in the pitch black, and we'd have dinner by lamplight. I had this beautiful Tiffany-style lamp on the dining table. Stained glass, with all these rich greens and reds. I bought it at an estate sale over in Westover. The woman running the sale had the most enormous collection of vintage brooches. I almost bought one shaped like a peacock, but I didn't have enough cash on me. You know, nobody carries cash anymore. It's all those plastic cards. When I go to the grocery store, the young people just tap their phones on the machine! It's like magic, or science fiction. I still write a check for my groceries. The cashier at the Piggly Wiggly—her name is Brenda, lovely girl, just had a baby boy—she's always so patient with me while I fill it out. But time of day? No, I don't really lose track of that. My stomach always tells me when it's time for lunch, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble learning new things, like remembering new names or how to use new devices?\n\nPatient: Learning new things? No, I wouldn't say that. I do just fine. I mean, my grandson Tommy got me one of those... what do you call it, a tablet? For Christmas. He wrapped it in this beautiful shiny silver foil paper. I always save the good wrapping paper, you know. My mother taught me that. We grew up when you didn't just throw things away! We'd actually iron the wrapping paper flat and fold it up to keep in the cedar chest. Oh, that chest smelled so wonderful. It sat right at the foot of my bed. I shared a room with my sister, Betty. Betty was always getting into my things, especially my makeup. She actually took my favorite red lipstick right before the spring dance in... oh, it must have been 1962. I was so mad at her! I had to wear this awful pale pink color that just washed me out completely. I did have a gorgeous blue dress, though, with a sweetheart neckline. My aunt made it for me. She was a seamstress downtown, right next to the old bakery that sold those wonderful almond cookies. I haven't had one of those in years. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, new things. No, I don't have any trouble, I just ask Tommy if I need help with the tablet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure who you are or what your role is?\n\nPatient: Unsure who I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly who I am. I'm Eleanor, Arthur's wife, Tommy's grandmother. I've always known my place in the world. Although, the world certainly has changed, hasn't it? I remember when being a mother and a wife was all a woman really needed to worry about. We had such a lovely neighborhood back then. The Johnsons lived next door, and Mrs. Johnson—Edith, her name was—she used to grow the most spectacular hydrangeas. Huge, blue blooms, bigger than my head! She told me her secret was putting rusty nails in the soil. Can you imagine? Rusty nails! Arthur used to keep a coffee can full of rusty nails in the garage. He never threw anything away, just like me with the wrapping paper. His workbench was always covered in sawdust and little bits of wire. He built a birdhouse once, painted it bright red to attract the cardinals. We used to sit on the porch with our iced tea and watch them. I always put a sprig of fresh mint in my iced tea. Mint grows like a weed, you know. If you don't keep it contained in a pot, it'll take over your whole garden before you can blink. Just like the ivy on the old library downtown. They had to tear that building down, which was a crying shame. It had those beautiful stone gargoyles on the roof. But no, I've never forgotten who I am. I'm still just me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure of your own identity or who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? About knowing who I am? Oh, it's alright, dear, I know you doctors have a lot of questions to get through. Arthur's doctor used to have this clipboard with pages and pages of questions. Dr. Henderson, his name was. He had the most magnificent mustache, curled right up at the ends like a man in a barbershop quartet. My father was in a barbershop quartet, actually! They called themselves the Melody Men. They'd practice in our living room on Thursday nights, and my mother would make them little cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off. She had a special silver tray she only used for company. I still have that tray, it's wrapped in felt in the dining room buffet. The buffet is real mahogany, they don't make furniture like that anymore. Now it's all that particle board you have to put together yourself with a tiny little wrench. Tommy put together a bookshelf for me last year and he was cursing a blue streak under his breath, though he thought I couldn't hear him. My hearing is still sharp as a tack, you know! I can hear the mail truck coming from three blocks away. But to answer your question again, no, I know exactly who I am. I haven't forgotten that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

She was a seamstress downtown, right next to the old bakery that sold those wonderful almond cookies. I haven't had one of those in years. But anyway, what was the question? Oh, new things. No, I don't have any trouble, I just ask Tommy if I need help with the tablet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure who you are or what your role is?\n\nPatient: Unsure who I am? Oh, heavens no. I know exactly who I am. I'm Eleanor, Arthur's wife, Tommy's grandmother. I've always known my place in the world. Although, the world certainly has changed, hasn't it? I remember when being a mother and a wife was all a woman really needed to worry about. We had such a lovely neighborhood back then. The Johnsons lived next door, and Mrs. Johnson—Edith, her name was—she used to grow the most spectacular hydrangeas. Huge, blue blooms, bigger than my head! She told me her secret was putting rusty nails in the soil. Can you imagine? Rusty nails! Arthur used to keep a coffee can full of rusty nails in the garage. He never threw anything away, just like me with the wrapping paper. His workbench was always covered in sawdust and little bits of wire. He built a birdhouse once, painted it bright red to attract the cardinals. We used to sit on the porch with our iced tea and watch them. I always put a sprig of fresh mint in my iced tea. Mint grows like a weed, you know. If you don't keep it contained in a pot, it'll take over your whole garden before you can blink. Just like the ivy on the old library downtown. They had to tear that building down, which was a crying shame. It had those beautiful stone gargoyles on the roof. But no, I've never forgotten who I am. I'm still just me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself unsure of your own identity or who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: I think you just asked me that, didn't you? About knowing who I am? Oh, it's alright, dear, I know you doctors have a lot of questions to get through. Arthur's doctor used to have this clipboard with pages and pages of questions. Dr. Henderson, his name was. He had the most magnificent mustache, curled right up at the ends like a man in a barbershop quartet. My father was in a barbershop quartet, actually! They called themselves the Melody Men. They'd practice in our living room on Thursday nights, and my mother would make them little cucumber sandwiches with the crusts cut off. She had a special silver tray she only used for company. I still have that tray, it's wrapped in felt in the dining room buffet. The buffet is real mahogany, they don't make furniture like that anymore. Now it's all that particle board you have to put together yourself with a tiny little wrench. Tommy put together a bookshelf for me last year and he was cursing a blue streak under his breath, though he thought I couldn't hear him. My hearing is still sharp as a tack, you know! I can hear the mail truck coming from three blocks away. But to answer your question again, no, I know exactly who I am. I haven't forgotten that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Find the right words? Oh, no, I don't think so. I've always been quite a talker, you know. My mother used to say I could talk the ears off a brass monkey! We actually had a brass monkey statue in the parlor when I was growing up. It was a funny little thing, holding a tiny umbrella. My father brought it back from his time in the Navy. He was stationed in the Pacific, you see. He brought back all sorts of fascinating things—a carved wooden box that smelled like sandalwood, and a silk kimono that was the most beautiful shade of emerald green. I wore it for Halloween once when I was twelve. I went as a princess from a faraway land. My mother was so worried I'd ruin the silk, but I was very careful. I've always been careful with nice things. It's like my china set, the one with the delicate rose pattern. I only bring it out for Easter and Thanksgiving. Thanksgiving is my favorite, though the turkey is always such a fuss. Last year, Tommy insisted on deep-frying it out in the yard. I was terrified he'd set the garage on fire! But it did turn out remarkably juicy. Anyway, what were we talking about? Words? No, I never have trouble finding the right words, dear. They just come right to me.
